People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess I'm here because... mostly I just don't feel like myself anymore. I stopped doing everything. I stopped hiking, coaching... playing my guitar. I have a Fender Stratocaster, sunburst finish. Bought it at a pawn shop off 4th street about ten years ago. The guy working there was missing half his left thumb, which you'd think would make it hard to work a cash register, but he was actually pretty fast. He told me it was a table saw accident. Table saws are incredibly dangerous if you don't use the guard, my uncle had one in his garage and he wouldn't let anyone near it... 

But anyway, I haven't picked the guitar up in months. I don't even call people back anymore. My phone just rings. Some days I just sit in that small rental of mine for hours. I look at the wall and I know there are things to do, but... I just can't make myself start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no injuries or anything. My physical health is fine. I haven't been sick. I mean, I have to pass a medical check for work anyway, doing security, so they check my blood pressure and all that. The doctor who does our clearance, he uses this really old blood pressure cuff. The kind you have to pump up by hand. 

My grandfather had one of those in his house, actually. He wasn't a doctor, he just liked to check his own pressure every morning after he drank his coffee. He drank it black, made in one of those old stovetop percolators. It always smelled completely burnt. You can't even really buy percolators anymore, everyone just uses those little plastic pods now. They're terrible for the environment, you know, all that plastic just sitting in landfills for a thousand years. The seagulls end up eating it. I saw a documentary about seagulls once, they're actually highly intelligent problem solvers... but yeah, no. Physically, I'm fine.

No, I... I don't think so. I've never felt like this before. It's like... it's like a heavy blanket, you know? Just sitting on me. I used to have this heavy wool blanket when I was a kid. Army surplus, I think. My dad bought it at this store downtown that sold old military gear. They had canteens, helmets, those little folding shovels... trenching tools, they call them. 

My dad used to say a good shovel was the most important thing you could own. He worked construction for thirty years, pouring concrete mostly. Concrete is fascinating if you really think about it. It's basically liquid rock that just... becomes solid. The Romans used it, you know. They mixed volcanic ash in theirs, which is why the Pantheon is still standing after two thousand years. Modern concrete doesn't last nearly as long because of the steel rebar inside it. The steel rusts and expands, and it cracks the concrete from the inside out. It's called concrete cancer. 

I saw a crack in the sidewalk outside my apartment building just the other day. It was shaped exactly like the state of Florida. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I've never had a period like this before. This is the first time.

I don't think so. I've never felt like this before. It's like... it's like a heavy blanket, you know? Just sitting on me. I used to have this heavy wool blanket when I was a kid. They had canteens, helmets, those little folding shovels... trenching tools, they call them. \n\nMy dad used to say a good shovel was the most important thing you could own. The Romans used it, you know. They mixed volcanic ash in theirs, which is why the Pantheon is still standing after two thousand years. Modern concrete doesn't last nearly as long because of the steel rebar inside it. The steel rusts and expands, and it cracks the concrete from the inside out. It's called concrete cancer. \n\nI saw a crack in the sidewalk outside my apartment building just the other day. It was shaped exactly like the state of Florida. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I've never had a period like this before. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... things have been unraveling since I moved into the smaller rental after my divorce. That was about a year and two months ago. The rental is over on Elm Street, you know, right near that old bakery that closed down. They used to make the best sourdough bread. The secret to good sourdough is the starter, obviously. Some bakeries keep their starter alive for decades. I read about a bakery in San Francisco that has a starter from the Gold Rush. 

The Gold Rush is wild when you think about it. All those people leaving everything behind, risking their lives, just for the chance to find shiny rocks in a river. My ex-wife loved shiny things. She had this necklace, silver with a little sapphire pendant. I bought it for her on our fifth anniversary. Sapphires are actually just corundum, same as rubies, just different trace elements. Rubies have chromium, sapphires have iron and titanium. Titanium is what they use in hip replacements because it's biocompatible. My neighbor in the new rental just got a hip replacement. He walks with a cane now. It's got a little tennis ball on the bottom so it doesn't scratch the floors. The floors in my place are that cheap laminate stuff that peels at the edges if you get it too wet... but yeah, it's been about a year and two months since I started feeling this way.

No, never. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. 

Hunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.

The floors in my place are that cheap laminate stuff that peels at the edges if you get it too wet... but yeah, it's been about a year and two months since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Just to be completely clear—before this started a year and two months ago, have you ever had a period of several weeks or longer where you felt depressed, hopeless, or lost interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, never. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\n[Last Question]\nOn most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? And how would you rate the intensity—is it mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I... I don't really know how to rate it. I guess I wouldn't call it sad. It's just... heavy. Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog.

People personifying weather patterns. 

But I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Just... stuck under the blanket.

chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this heavy blanket feeling where you can't motivate yourself and have lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... things have been unraveling since I moved into the smaller rental after my divorce. That was about a year and two months ago. The rental is over on Elm Street, you know, right near that old bakery that closed down. They used to make the best sourdough bread. The secret to good sourdough is the starter, obviously. Some bakeries keep their starter alive for decades. I read about a bakery in San Francisco that has a starter from the Gold Rush. \n\nThe Gold Rush is wild when you think about it. All those people leaving everything behind, risking their lives, just for the chance to find shiny rocks in a river. My ex-wife loved shiny things. She had this necklace, silver with a little sapphire pendant. I bought it for her on our fifth anniversary. Sapphires are actually just corundum, same as rubies, just different trace elements. Rubies have chromium, sapphires have iron and titanium. Titanium is what they use in hip replacements because it's biocompatible. My neighbor in the new rental just got a hip replacement. He walks with a cane now. It's got a little tennis ball on the bottom so it doesn't scratch the floors. The floors in my place are that cheap laminate stuff that peels at the edges if you get it too wet... but yeah, it's been about a year and two months since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Just to be completely clear—before this started a year and two months ago, have you ever had a period of several weeks or longer where you felt depressed, hopeless, or lost interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, never. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\nDoctor: On most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? And how would you rate the intensity—is it mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know how to rate it. I guess I wouldn't call it sad. It's just... heavy. Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog. \n\nFog is actually just a low-lying cloud, you know. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Just... stuck under the blanket.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you think about things you used to enjoy—like hiking or playing guitar—do you feel any desire or pleasure when you imagine doing them, or does the idea of doing them feel completely empty and joyless to you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like I'm empty. I stopped hiking, and I used to coach, but I stopped that too. I don't even play my guitar. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. 

Different woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. 

But anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.

He worked construction for thirty years, pouring concrete mostly. Concrete is fascinating if you really think about it. It's basically liquid rock that just... becomes solid. The Romans used it, you know. They mixed volcanic ash in theirs, which is why the Pantheon is still standing after two thousand years. Modern concrete doesn't last nearly as long because of the steel rebar inside it. The steel rusts and expands, and it cracks the concrete from the inside out. It's called concrete cancer. \n\nI saw a crack in the sidewalk outside my apartment building just the other day. It was shaped exactly like the state of Florida. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I've never had a period like this before. This is the first time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this heavy blanket feeling where you can't motivate yourself and have lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... things have been unraveling since I moved into the smaller rental after my divorce. That was about a year and two months ago. The rental is over on Elm Street, you know, right near that old bakery that closed down. They used to make the best sourdough bread. The secret to good sourdough is the starter, obviously. Some bakeries keep their starter alive for decades. I read about a bakery in San Francisco that has a starter from the Gold Rush. \n\nThe Gold Rush is wild when you think about it. All those people leaving everything behind, risking their lives, just for the chance to find shiny rocks in a river. My ex-wife loved shiny things. She had this necklace, silver with a little sapphire pendant. I bought it for her on our fifth anniversary. Sapphires are actually just corundum, same as rubies, just different trace elements. Rubies have chromium, sapphires have iron and titanium. Titanium is what they use in hip replacements because it's biocompatible. My neighbor in the new rental just got a hip replacement. He walks with a cane now. It's got a little tennis ball on the bottom so it doesn't scratch the floors. The floors in my place are that cheap laminate stuff that peels at the edges if you get it too wet... but yeah, it's been about a year and two months since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Just to be completely clear—before this started a year and two months ago, have you ever had a period of several weeks or longer where you felt depressed, hopeless, or lost interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, never. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\nDoctor: On most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? And how would you rate the intensity—is it mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know how to rate it. I guess I wouldn't call it sad. It's just... heavy. Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog. \n\nFog is actually just a low-lying cloud, you know. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Just... stuck under the blanket.\n\nDoctor: When you think about things you used to enjoy—like hiking or playing guitar—do you feel any desire or pleasure when you imagine doing them, or does the idea of doing them feel completely empty and joyless to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like I'm empty. I stopped hiking, and I used to coach, but I stopped that too. I don't even play my guitar. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don't really know, to be honest. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. 

Darkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. "God does not play dice with the universe," he said. 

But it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.

I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\nDoctor: On most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? And how would you rate the intensity—is it mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know how to rate it. I guess I wouldn't call it sad. It's just... heavy. Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog. \n\nFog is actually just a low-lying cloud, you know. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Just... stuck under the blanket.\n\nDoctor: When you think about things you used to enjoy—like hiking or playing guitar—do you feel any desire or pleasure when you imagine doing them, or does the idea of doing them feel completely empty and joyless to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like I'm empty. I stopped hiking, and I used to coach, but I stopped that too. I don't even play my guitar. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year? Are you sleeping more, less, or about the same as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. \n\nDarkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Exhibits psychomotor retardation (sits for hours unable to initiate activity) and social withdrawal (doesn't return calls). Sleep changes unclear but patient appears indifferent to sleep patterns. Added F32.1 (moderate) and F32.2 (severe) as severity remains to be clarified; F32.9 (unspecified) retained pending further assessment.", "is_final": false}

Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year? Are you sleeping more, less, or about the same as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. \n\nDarkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver this past year, have you noticed any changes in your appetite or your weight? Have you been eating more, less, or about the same as before?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / protective_service_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven't really noticed any changes, to be honest. I mean, I eat when I eat. I don't think I've gained or lost any weight. 

Weight is a funny concept though, because it changes depending on where you are. Like, if you were on the moon, you'd weigh about one-sixth of what you weigh here on Earth because the gravity is weaker. Gravity is what keeps everything anchored. The tides, the moon orbiting the Earth, the Earth orbiting the sun. The sun is massive. It accounts for like 99.8% of all the mass in our solar system. The rest of it is just Jupiter and a bunch of dust, basically. 

Speaking of dust, did you know that a lot of the dust in your house is actually dead skin cells? It's kind of gross when you think about it. We're just constantly shedding pieces of ourselves everywhere we go. Like snakes shedding their skin. Snakes are interesting creatures. Some of them can go months without eating a single thing. They just slow their metabolism way down. I saw a documentary about anaconda snakes in the Amazon once. The Amazon rainforest produces 20% of the world's oxygen. They call it the lungs of the Earth. But they're cutting it all down for cattle ranching. People eat so much beef. 

But yeah, my appetite? I don't think it's changed. I just eat whatever, whenever. Same as always, I guess.

I read about a bakery in San Francisco that has a starter from the Gold Rush. \n\nThe Gold Rush is wild when you think about it. All those people leaving everything behind, risking their lives, just for the chance to find shiny rocks in a river. My ex-wife loved shiny things. Rubies have chromium, sapphires have iron and titanium. Titanium is what they use in hip replacements because it's biocompatible. My neighbor in the new rental just got a hip replacement. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\nDoctor: On most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog. \n\nFog is actually just a low-lying cloud, you know. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. No, I don't think anyone has said anything like that. I haven't noticed feeling slow or sluggish. 

Slugs are fascinating, though. Have you ever watched a slug move? They secrete this mucus that lets them glide over rough surfaces, even razor blades, without getting cut. It's a non-Newtonian fluid. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. 

Glass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. 

But no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.

Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed any changes in your appetite or your weight? Have you been eating more, less, or about the same as before?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any changes, to be honest. I mean, I eat when I eat. I don't think I've gained or lost any weight. \n\nWeight is a funny concept though, because it changes depending on where you are. Like, if you were on the moon, you'd weigh about one-sixth of what you weigh here on Earth because the gravity is weaker. Gravity is what keeps everything anchored. The tides, the moon orbiting the Earth, the Earth orbiting the sun. The sun is massive. It accounts for like 99.8% of all the mass in our solar system. The rest of it is just Jupiter and a bunch of dust, basically. \n\nSpeaking of dust, did you know that a lot of the dust in your house is actually dead skin cells? It's kind of gross when you think about it. We're just constantly shedding pieces of ourselves everywhere we go. Like snakes shedding their skin. Snakes are interesting creatures. Some of them can go months without eating a single thing. They just slow their metabolism way down. I saw a documentary about anaconda snakes in the Amazon once. The Amazon rainforest produces 20% of the world's oxygen. They call it the lungs of the Earth. But they're cutting it all down for cattle ranching. People eat so much beef. \n\nBut yeah, my appetite? I don't think it's changed. I just eat whatever, whenever. Same as always, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have people around you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or have you felt like your body is sluggish or hard to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone has said anything like that. I haven't noticed feeling slow or sluggish. \n\nSlugs are fascinating, though. Have you ever watched a slug move? They secrete this mucus that lets them glide over rough surfaces, even razor blades, without getting cut. It's a non-Newtonian fluid. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. \n\nGlass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. \n\nBut no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's kind of gross when you think about it. We're just constantly shedding pieces of ourselves everywhere we go. Like snakes shedding their skin. Snakes are interesting creatures. Some of them can go months without eating a single thing. They just slow their metabolism way down. I saw a documentary about anaconda snakes in the Amazon once. The Amazon rainforest produces 20% of the world's oxygen. They call it the lungs of the Earth. But they're cutting it all down for cattle ranching. People eat so much beef. \n\nBut yeah, my appetite? I don't think it's changed. I just eat whatever, whenever. Same as always, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have people around you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or have you felt like your body is sluggish or hard to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone has said anything like that. I haven't noticed feeling slow or sluggish. \n\nSlugs are fascinating, though. Have you ever watched a slug move? They secrete this mucus that lets them glide over rough surfaces, even razor blades, without getting cut. It's a non-Newtonian fluid. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. \n\nGlass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. \n\nBut no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver this past year, have you felt guilty about things, or felt like you're worthless, or blamed yourself for problems?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / protective_service_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? No, I don't... I haven't really felt guilty about anything. Or worthless. I'm just me. 

Worth is a strange thing to measure, isn't it? Like, how do you put a value on something? We use money, but money isn't even real anymore. It used to be backed by gold—the gold standard. Fort Knox, all those bricks of gold sitting in a vault. But now it's just fiat currency. It only has value because the government says it does, and because we all agree to pretend it does. Most of it is just numbers on a computer screen anyway. 

Computers are everywhere now. The smartphone in your pocket has more processing power than the computers NASA used to send Apollo 11 to the moon. They landed on the moon with 4 kilobytes of RAM. Can you imagine? Now people complain if their phone takes an extra second to load a video of a cat playing the piano. Pianos have 88 keys. 52 white, 36 black. Beethoven went deaf but he still composed the Ninth Symphony. He actually sawed the legs off his piano so he could sit on the floor and feel the vibrations of the notes through the wood. Wood is a great conductor of sound. That's why violins are made of wood. Stradivarius violins are worth millions because of the density of the wood they used back then during the Little Ice Age. 

But yeah, I don't feel worthless. I don't really blame myself for things. It just is what it is.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... mostly I just don't feel like myself anymore. I stopped doing everything. The guy working there was missing half his left thumb, which you'd think would make it hard to work a cash register, but he was actually pretty fast. He told me it was a table saw accident. Table saws are incredibly dangerous if you don't use the guard, my uncle had one in his garage and he wouldn't let anyone near it... \n\nBut anyway, I haven't picked the guitar up in months. My physical health is fine. I haven't been sick. I mean, I have to pass a medical check for work anyway, doing security, so they check my blood pressure and all that. The doctor who does our clearance, he uses this really old blood pressure cuff. The kind you have to pump up by hand. \n\nMy grandfather had one of those in his house, actually. They had canteens, helmets, those little folding shovels... trenching tools, they call them. \n\nMy dad used to say a good shovel was the most important thing you could own. He worked construction for thirty years, pouring concrete mostly. Concrete is fascinating if you really think about it. It's basically liquid rock that just... becomes solid. The Romans used it, you know. They mixed volcanic ash in theirs, which is why the Pantheon is still standing after two thousand years. Modern concrete doesn't last nearly as long because of the steel rebar inside it. The steel rusts and expands, and it cracks the concrete from the inside out. It's called concrete cancer. \n\nI saw a crack in the sidewalk outside my apartment building just the other day. It was shaped exactly like the state of Florida. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I've never had a period like this before. This is the first time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this heavy blanket feeling where you can't motivate yourself and have lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... things have been unraveling since I moved into the smaller rental after my divorce. That was about a year and two months ago. The rental is over on Elm Street, you know, right near that old bakery that closed down. They used to make the best sourdough bread. The secret to good sourdough is the starter, obviously. Some bakeries keep their starter alive for decades. I read about a bakery in San Francisco that has a starter from the Gold Rush. \n\nThe Gold Rush is wild when you think about it. All those people leaving everything behind, risking their lives, just for the chance to find shiny rocks in a river. My ex-wife loved shiny things. She had this necklace, silver with a little sapphire pendant. I bought it for her on our fifth anniversary. Sapphires are actually just corundum, same as rubies, just different trace elements. Rubies have chromium, sapphires have iron and titanium. Titanium is what they use in hip replacements because it's biocompatible. My neighbor in the new rental just got a hip replacement. He walks with a cane now. It's got a little tennis ball on the bottom so it doesn't scratch the floors. The floors in my place are that cheap laminate stuff that peels at the edges if you get it too wet... but yeah, it's been about a year and two months since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Just to be completely clear—before this started a year and two months ago, have you ever had a period of several weeks or longer where you felt depressed, hopeless, or lost interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, never. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\nDoctor: On most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? And how would you rate the intensity—is it mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know how to rate it. I guess I wouldn't call it sad. It's just... heavy. Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog. \n\nFog is actually just a low-lying cloud, you know. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Just... stuck under the blanket.\n\nDoctor: When you think about things you used to enjoy—like hiking or playing guitar—do you feel any desire or pleasure when you imagine doing them, or does the idea of doing them feel completely empty and joyless to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like I'm empty. I stopped hiking, and I used to coach, but I stopped that too. I don't even play my guitar. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year? Are you sleeping more, less, or about the same as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. \n\nDarkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed any changes in your appetite or your weight? Have you been eating more, less, or about the same as before?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any changes, to be honest. I mean, I eat when I eat. I don't think I've gained or lost any weight. \n\nWeight is a funny concept though, because it changes depending on where you are. Like, if you were on the moon, you'd weigh about one-sixth of what you weigh here on Earth because the gravity is weaker. Gravity is what keeps everything anchored. The tides, the moon orbiting the Earth, the Earth orbiting the sun. The sun is massive. It accounts for like 99.8% of all the mass in our solar system. The rest of it is just Jupiter and a bunch of dust, basically. \n\nSpeaking of dust, did you know that a lot of the dust in your house is actually dead skin cells? It's kind of gross when you think about it. We're just constantly shedding pieces of ourselves everywhere we go. Like snakes shedding their skin. Snakes are interesting creatures. Some of them can go months without eating a single thing. They just slow their metabolism way down. I saw a documentary about anaconda snakes in the Amazon once. The Amazon rainforest produces 20% of the world's oxygen. They call it the lungs of the Earth. But they're cutting it all down for cattle ranching. People eat so much beef. \n\nBut yeah, my appetite? I don't think it's changed. I just eat whatever, whenever. Same as always, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have people around you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or have you felt like your body is sluggish or hard to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone has said anything like that. I haven't noticed feeling slow or sluggish. \n\nSlugs are fascinating, though. Have you ever watched a slug move? They secrete this mucus that lets them glide over rough surfaces, even razor blades, without getting cut. It's a non-Newtonian fluid. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. \n\nGlass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. \n\nBut no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you felt guilty about things, or felt like you're worthless, or blamed yourself for problems?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I haven't really felt guilty about anything. Or worthless. I'm just me. \n\nWorth is a strange thing to measure, isn't it? Like, how do you put a value on something? We use money, but money isn't even real anymore. It used to be backed by gold—the gold standard. Fort Knox, all those bricks of gold sitting in a vault. But now it's just fiat currency. It only has value because the government says it does, and because we all agree to pretend it does. Most of it is just numbers on a computer screen anyway. \n\nComputers are everywhere now. The smartphone in your pocket has more processing power than the computers NASA used to send Apollo 11 to the moon. They landed on the moon with 4 kilobytes of RAM. Can you imagine? Now people complain if their phone takes an extra second to load a video of a cat playing the piano. Pianos have 88 keys. 52 white, 36 black. Beethoven went deaf but he still composed the Ninth Symphony. He actually sawed the legs off his piano so he could sit on the floor and feel the vibrations of the notes through the wood. Wood is a great conductor of sound. That's why violins are made of wood. Stradivarius violins are worth millions because of the density of the wood they used back then during the Little Ice Age. \n\nBut yeah, I don't feel worthless. I don't really blame myself for things. It just is what it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like I'm empty. I stopped hiking, and I used to coach, but I stopped that too. I don't even play my guitar. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year? Are you sleeping more, less, or about the same as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. \n\nDarkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed any changes in your appetite or your weight? Have you been eating more, less, or about the same as before?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any changes, to be honest. I mean, I eat when I eat. I don't think I've gained or lost any weight. \n\nWeight is a funny concept though, because it changes depending on where you are. Like, if you were on the moon, you'd weigh about one-sixth of what you weigh here on Earth because the gravity is weaker. Gravity is what keeps everything anchored. The tides, the moon orbiting the Earth, the Earth orbiting the sun. The sun is massive. It accounts for like 99.8% of all the mass in our solar system. The rest of it is just Jupiter and a bunch of dust, basically. \n\nSpeaking of dust, did you know that a lot of the dust in your house is actually dead skin cells? It's kind of gross when you think about it. We're just constantly shedding pieces of ourselves everywhere we go. Like snakes shedding their skin. Snakes are interesting creatures. Some of them can go months without eating a single thing. They just slow their metabolism way down. I saw a documentary about anaconda snakes in the Amazon once. The Amazon rainforest produces 20% of the world's oxygen. They call it the lungs of the Earth. But they're cutting it all down for cattle ranching. People eat so much beef. \n\nBut yeah, my appetite? I don't think it's changed. I just eat whatever, whenever. Same as always, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have people around you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or have you felt like your body is sluggish or hard to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone has said anything like that. I haven't noticed feeling slow or sluggish. \n\nSlugs are fascinating, though. Have you ever watched a slug move? They secrete this mucus that lets them glide over rough surfaces, even razor blades, without getting cut. It's a non-Newtonian fluid. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. \n\nGlass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. \n\nBut no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you felt guilty about things, or felt like you're worthless, or blamed yourself for problems?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I haven't really felt guilty about anything. Or worthless. I'm just me. \n\nWorth is a strange thing to measure, isn't it? Like, how do you put a value on something? It only has value because the government says it does, and because we all agree to pretend it does. Most of it is just numbers on a computer screen anyway. \n\nComputers are everywhere now. The smartphone in your pocket has more processing power than the computers NASA used to send Apollo 11 to the moon. They landed on the moon with 4 kilobytes of RAM. Can you imagine? Now people complain if their phone takes an extra second to load a video of a cat playing the piano. Pianos have 88 keys. 52 white, 36 black. Beethoven went deaf but he still composed the Ninth Symphony. He actually sawed the legs off his piano so he could sit on the floor and feel the vibrations of the notes through the wood. Wood is a great conductor of sound. That's why violins are made of wood. Stradivarius violins are worth millions because of the density of the wood they used back then during the Little Ice Age. \n\nBut yeah, I don't feel worthless. I don't really blame myself for things. Before the divorce? No, I don't... I don't remember ever feeling down or unmotivated back then. I was always pretty focused, just working a lot. I do security, so you have to stay alert. 

Alertness is all about adrenaline, really. Your adrenal glands sit right on top of your kidneys like little hats. Kidneys are amazing, they filter about 150 quarts of blood every single day. That's a lot of liquid. Most of the Earth is liquid, if you think about the oceans. The Pacific Ocean is so big that it actually contains its own antipodes—like, if you dug a hole straight through the center of the Earth from certain parts of the Pacific, you'd come out in another part of the Pacific. The Mariana Trench is the deepest part. It's so deep that the water pressure would crush a nuclear submarine like an empty soda can. Aluminum cans are infinitely recyclable, though. You can melt one down and put a new can back on the grocery shelf in about 60 days. Recycling is a huge industry, but a lot of the plastic just ends up in the ocean anyway, floating around in these massive garbage patches. 

But yeah, before I moved, I never had any weeks or months where I felt down. It was just a normal life back then.

My physical health is fine. I haven't been sick. I mean, I have to pass a medical check for work anyway, doing security, so they check my blood pressure and all that. The doctor who does our clearance, he uses this really old blood pressure cuff. The kind you have to pump up by hand. \n\nMy grandfather had one of those in his house, actually. He wasn't a doctor, he just liked to check his own pressure every morning after he drank his coffee. He drank it black, made in one of those old stovetop percolators. It always smelled completely burnt. You can't even really buy percolators anymore, everyone just uses those little plastic pods now. They're terrible for the environment, you know, all that plastic just sitting in landfills for a thousand years. The seagulls end up eating it. I saw a documentary about seagulls once, they're actually highly intelligent problem solvers... but yeah, no. Physically, I'm fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this before in your life—where you lost interest in things you normally enjoyed and felt unable to motivate yourself—or is this the first time this has happened to you?\n\nPatient: No, I...
