Army surplus, I think. My dad bought it at this store downtown that sold old military gear. They had canteens, helmets, those little folding shovels... trenching tools, they call them. \n\nMy dad used to say a good shovel was the most important thing you could own. He worked construction for thirty years, pouring concrete mostly. Concrete is fascinating if you really think about it. It's basically liquid rock that just... becomes solid. The Romans used it, you know. They mixed volcanic ash in theirs, which is why the Pantheon is still standing after two thousand years. Modern concrete doesn't last nearly as long because of the steel rebar inside it. The steel rusts and expands, and it cracks the concrete from the inside out. It's called concrete cancer. \n\nI saw a crack in the sidewalk outside my apartment building just the other day. It was shaped exactly like the state of Florida. But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I've never had a period like this before. This is the first time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this heavy blanket feeling where you can't motivate yourself and have lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... things have been unraveling since I moved into the smaller rental after my divorce. That was about a year and two months ago. The rental is over on Elm Street, you know, right near that old bakery that closed down. They used to make the best sourdough bread. The secret to good sourdough is the starter, obviously. Some bakeries keep their starter alive for decades. I read about a bakery in San Francisco that has a starter from the Gold Rush. \n\nThe Gold Rush is wild when you think about it. All those people leaving everything behind, risking their lives, just for the chance to find shiny rocks in a river. My ex-wife loved shiny things. She had this necklace, silver with a little sapphire pendant. I bought it for her on our fifth anniversary. Sapphires are actually just corundum, same as rubies, just different trace elements. Rubies have chromium, sapphires have iron and titanium. Titanium is what they use in hip replacements because it's biocompatible. My neighbor in the new rental just got a hip replacement. He walks with a cane now. It's got a little tennis ball on the bottom so it doesn't scratch the floors. The floors in my place are that cheap laminate stuff that peels at the edges if you get it too wet... but yeah, it's been about a year and two months since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Just to be completely clear—before this started a year and two months ago, have you ever had a period of several weeks or longer where you felt depressed, hopeless, or lost interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, never. I mean, I told you, right? This is the first time. Before I moved, before the divorce, I was fine. I used to go fishing on the weekends. Not all the time, but, you know, enough. I had this really nice tackle box, the kind with the little cantilever trays that fold out. It was green. Hunter green, I think they call it. \n\nHunters wear a lot of orange now, though. Blaze orange, so they don't shoot each other in the woods. It's funny how deer can't see orange. They're dichromatic, so they only see blues and greens, mostly. That's why tigers are orange, actually. To a deer, a tiger just looks green, like the jungle. It's perfect camouflage if your prey is colorblind. I learned that from a podcast. I used to listen to a lot of podcasts on my commute, before... well, before everything. Commuting is such a strange concept. Millions of people just sitting in metal boxes, burning fossil fuels, all going to the same place at the exact same time. It's so inefficient. If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. Just this past year.\n\nDoctor: On most days over this past year, would you say your mood has been depressed or sad? And how would you rate the intensity—is it mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know how to rate it. I guess I wouldn't call it sad. It's just... heavy. Like I said about the blanket. It's just there, every day. Most days, yeah. I don't know if it's mild or severe or whatever, it's just... it's like a fog. \n\nFog is actually just a low-lying cloud, you know. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Just... stuck under the blanket.\n\nDoctor: When you think about things you used to enjoy—like hiking or playing guitar—do you feel any desire or pleasure when you imagine doing them, or does the idea of doing them feel completely empty and joyless to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like I'm empty. I stopped hiking, and I used to coach, but I stopped that too. I don't even play my guitar. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. I don't call people back. Some days I just sit in that small rental for hours. I know there are things I should be doing, things I need to do, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's just empty.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over this past year? Are you sleeping more, less, or about the same as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. \n\nDarkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It's a thought experiment, but it's crazy to think about. I had a cat once, when I was a kid. A tabby named Whiskers. Very original name, I know. He used to sleep at the foot of my bed every night. But... yeah, I'm not really sure about my sleep these days. I haven't really noticed a difference.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed any changes in your appetite or your weight? Have you been eating more, less, or about the same as before?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any changes, to be honest. I mean, I eat when I eat. I don't think I've gained or lost any weight. \n\nWeight is a funny concept though, because it changes depending on where you are. Like, if you were on the moon, you'd weigh about one-sixth of what you weigh here on Earth because the gravity is weaker. Gravity is what keeps everything anchored. The tides, the moon orbiting the Earth, the Earth orbiting the sun. The sun is massive. It accounts for like 99.8% of all the mass in our solar system. The rest of it is just Jupiter and a bunch of dust, basically. \n\nSpeaking of dust, did you know that a lot of the dust in your house is actually dead skin cells? It's kind of gross when you think about it. We're just constantly shedding pieces of ourselves everywhere we go. Like snakes shedding their skin. Snakes are interesting creatures. Some of them can go months without eating a single thing. They just slow their metabolism way down. I saw a documentary about anaconda snakes in the Amazon once. The Amazon rainforest produces 20% of the world's oxygen. They call it the lungs of the Earth. But they're cutting it all down for cattle ranching. People eat so much beef. \n\nBut yeah, my appetite? I don't think it's changed. I just eat whatever, whenever. Same as always, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have people around you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or have you felt like your body is sluggish or hard to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think anyone has said anything like that. I haven't noticed feeling slow or sluggish. \n\nSlugs are fascinating, though. Have you ever watched a slug move? They secrete this mucus that lets them glide over rough surfaces, even razor blades, without getting cut. It's a non-Newtonian fluid. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. \n\nGlass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. \n\nBut no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you felt guilty about things, or felt like you're worthless, or blamed yourself for problems?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I haven't really felt guilty about anything. Or worthless. I'm just me. \n\nWorth is a strange thing to measure, isn't it? Like, how do you put a value on something? We use money, but money isn't even real anymore. It used to be backed by gold—the gold standard. Fort Knox, all those bricks of gold sitting in a vault. But now it's just fiat currency. It only has value because the government says it does, and because we all agree to pretend it does. Most of it is just numbers on a computer screen anyway. \n\nComputers are everywhere now. The smartphone in your pocket has more processing power than the computers NASA used to send Apollo 11 to the moon. They landed on the moon with 4 kilobytes of RAM. Can you imagine? Now people complain if their phone takes an extra second to load a video of a cat playing the piano. Pianos have 88 keys. 52 white, 36 black. Beethoven went deaf but he still composed the Ninth Symphony. He actually sawed the legs off his piano so he could sit on the floor and feel the vibrations of the notes through the wood. Wood is a great conductor of sound. That's why violins are made of wood. Stradivarius violins are worth millions because of the density of the wood they used back then during the Little Ice Age. \n\nBut yeah, I don't feel worthless. I don't really blame myself for things. It just is what it is.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this is the first time you've felt this way, and it started around the time of your divorce a year and two months ago. But before the divorce, going back further—say, in the years before you moved—did you ever have stretches of weeks or months where you felt down or unmotivated, even if it wasn't as intense as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? No, I don't... I don't remember ever feeling down or unmotivated back then. I was always pretty focused, just working a lot. I do security, so you have to stay alert. \n\nAlertness is all about adrenaline, really. Your adrenal glands sit right on top of your kidneys like little hats. Kidneys are amazing, they filter about 150 quarts of blood every single day. That's a lot of liquid. Most of the Earth is liquid, if you think about the oceans. The Pacific Ocean is so big that it actually contains its own antipodes—like, if you dug a hole straight through the center of the Earth from certain parts of the Pacific, you'd come out in another part of the Pacific. The Mariana Trench is the deepest part. It's so deep that the water pressure would crush a nuclear submarine like an empty soda can. Aluminum cans are infinitely recyclable, though. You can melt one down and put a new can back on the grocery shelf in about 60 days. Recycling is a huge industry, but a lot of the plastic just ends up in the ocean anyway, floating around in these massive garbage patches. \n\nBut yeah, before I moved, I never had any weeks or months where I felt down. It was just a normal life back then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",

}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unusually energetic? Like, needing less sleep? No, I've never really had anything like that. I usually sleep pretty standard hours. Sleep is important. 

Well, except for dolphins. Did you know dolphins only sleep with half their brain at a time? It's called unihemispheric slow-wave sleep. They have to do that so they don't drown, because they have to consciously think about coming up to the surface to breathe. They don't have an automatic breathing reflex like we do. Reflexes are wild. Like when the doctor hits your knee with that little rubber hammer and your leg kicks out. That signal doesn't even go all the way to your brain, it just goes to your spinal cord and bounces right back. It's a survival mechanism. Spiders have crazy survival mechanisms, too. Some of them can actually detach their own legs if a predator grabs them, and then they just grow them back later when they molt. Molting sounds so uncomfortable, just crawling out of your own skeleton. Cicadas do that. They stay underground for 17 years just drinking tree sap, and then millions of them come out all at once, make a huge racket, and die a few weeks later. 

But no, I've never had a week where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just regular sleep for me.

Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. Sleep is... well, it's just sleep, isn't it? I haven't really kept track of the hours or anything. I just close my eyes when it's dark. \n\nDarkness is interesting, though. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. That means it acts like a liquid sometimes and a solid other times, depending on the pressure. Ketchup is a non-Newtonian fluid, too. That's why you have to hit the bottom of the glass bottle to get it to flow. \n\nGlass is actually a liquid, you know, just a really, really slow-moving one. If you look at the windows in really old churches, they're thicker at the bottom because the glass has been slowly flowing downward for centuries. Stained glass is beautiful. They used to use gold and silver nanoparticles to get those bright red and yellow colors. Nanotechnology isn't even a new thing, we just didn't know we were doing it back then. Now they're trying to use nanobots to cure cancer. Tiny little robots swimming in your bloodstream. It sounds like science fiction, but it's real. \n\nBut no, I'm not moving slow. Just regular, I guess. I don't really see people enough for them to notice anyway.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you felt guilty about things, or felt like you're worthless, or blamed yourself for problems?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I haven't really felt guilty about anything. Or worthless. I'm just me. \n\nWorth is a strange thing to measure, isn't it? Like, how do you put a value on something? We use money, but money isn't even real anymore. It used to be backed by gold—the gold standard. Fort Knox, all those bricks of gold sitting in a vault. But now it's just fiat currency. It only has value because the government says it does, and because we all agree to pretend it does. Most of it is just numbers on a computer screen anyway. \n\nComputers are everywhere now. The smartphone in your pocket has more processing power than the computers NASA used to send Apollo 11 to the moon. They landed on the moon with 4 kilobytes of RAM. Can you imagine? Now people complain if their phone takes an extra second to load a video of a cat playing the piano. Pianos have 88 keys. 52 white, 36 black. Beethoven went deaf but he still composed the Ninth Symphony. He actually sawed the legs off his piano so he could sit on the floor and feel the vibrations of the notes through the wood. Wood is a great conductor of sound. That's why violins are made of wood. Stradivarius violins are worth millions because of the density of the wood they used back then during the Little Ice Age. \n\nBut yeah, I don't feel worthless. I don't really blame myself for things. It just is what it is.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this is the first time you've felt this way, and it started around the time of your divorce a year and two months ago. But before the divorce, going back further—say, in the years before you moved—did you ever have stretches of weeks or months where you felt down or unmotivated, even if it wasn't as intense as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? No, I don't... I don't remember ever feeling down or unmotivated back then. I was always pretty focused, just working a lot. I do security, so you have to stay alert. \n\nAlertness is all about adrenaline, really. Your adrenal glands sit right on top of your kidneys like little hats. Kidneys are amazing, they filter about 150 quarts of blood every single day. That's a lot of liquid. Most of the Earth is liquid, if you think about the oceans. The Pacific Ocean is so big that it actually contains its own antipodes—like, if you dug a hole straight through the center of the Earth from certain parts of the Pacific, you'd come out in another part of the Pacific. The Mariana Trench is the deepest part. It's so deep that the water pressure would crush a nuclear submarine like an empty soda can. Aluminum cans are infinitely recyclable, though. You can melt one down and put a new can back on the grocery shelf in about 60 days. Recycling is a huge industry, but a lot of the plastic just ends up in the ocean anyway, floating around in these massive garbage patches. \n\nBut yeah, before I moved, I never had any weeks or months where I felt down. It was just a normal life back then.\n\nDoctor: Over your lifetime, have you ever had a period—maybe a week or two or longer—where you felt unusually energetic, confident, or 'up,' and needed much less sleep than normal but still felt rested?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic? Like, needing less sleep? No, I've never really had anything like that. I usually sleep pretty standard hours. Sleep is important. \n\nWell, except for dolphins. Did you know dolphins only sleep with half their brain at a time? It's called unihemispheric slow-wave sleep. They have to do that so they don't drown, because they have to consciously think about coming up to the surface to breathe. They don't have an automatic breathing reflex like we do. Reflexes are wild. Like when the doctor hits your knee with that little rubber hammer and your leg kicks out. That signal doesn't even go all the way to your brain, it just goes to your spinal cord and bounces right back. It's a survival mechanism. Spiders have crazy survival mechanisms, too. Some of them can actually detach their own legs if a predator grabs them, and then they just grow them back later when they molt. Molting sounds so uncomfortable, just crawling out of your own skeleton. Cicadas do that. They stay underground for 17 years just drinking tree sap, and then millions of them come out all at once, make a huge racket, and die a few weeks later. \n\nBut no, I've never had a week where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just regular sleep for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Harming myself? No, no, I haven't wanted to die or anything like that. I've never really had those kinds of thoughts. 

Thoughts are basically just electrical impulses, right? Like little sparks firing across synapses in your brain. Electricity is fascinating. Thomas Edison gets all the credit for the lightbulb, but Nikola Tesla was the one who really understood alternating current. Tesla was obsessed with the number three. Helium is what makes balloons float, but we're actually running out of it on Earth. Once it escapes into the atmosphere, it just drifts off into space forever. Space is a vacuum, so sound can't travel through it. If a star explodes, it's completely silent.

But no, to answer your question, I haven't had any thoughts about dying or hurting myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Racing ideas? No, I don't think my mind has ever felt like it was racing like that. I mean, I think a lot, but not racing. I don't take on a bunch of projects either. I just do my job. 

Jobs are funny things, really. We spend so much of our lives doing them. The word "salary" actually comes from the Latin word for salt, because Roman soldiers were sometimes paid in salt. Salt was super valuable back then. It preserves meat. Before refrigeration, if you didn't have salt, your food would just rot. Rotting is just bacteria breaking things down. Bacteria are everywhere. There are more bacteria cells in your body than human cells. You're basically just a walking ecosystem for microbes. Some of them live in extreme environments, like those hydrothermal vents at the bottom of the ocean. They call them extremophiles. They survive on chemicals instead of sunlight. Sunlight takes about eight minutes to reach the Earth. So if the sun just disappeared right now, we wouldn't even know it for eight minutes. We'd just be walking around in the daylight, totally oblivious. 

But no, I've never had a time where I felt unusually confident or took on a lot of projects. Just my regular routine.

If we just staggered work hours, there wouldn't be any traffic at all. But... wait, no. Like I said, I've never felt like this before. It happens when the air temperature drops to the dew point and the invisible water vapor condenses into tiny liquid water droplets. The Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco gets a lot of fog. They call it Karl. The locals actually named the fog Karl. They even made a Twitter account for it. Social media is bizarre. People personifying weather patterns. \n\nBut I guess humans have always done that, right? The Greeks had gods for everything. Zeus for lightning, Poseidon for the ocean. The ocean is terrifying. We know more about the surface of the moon than we do about the bottom of the ocean. There are fish down there that have their own light bulbs attached to their heads. Anglerfish. They use the light to attract prey in the pitch black. It's called bioluminescence. Fireflies do it too. I used to catch fireflies in a jar when I was a kid. You have to poke holes in the lid so they can breathe, otherwise they die. But... yeah, I don't know if I'd say I'm sad. Guitars are really fascinating when you think about the physics of them. The tension on those strings is incredible. A standard set of acoustic guitar strings puts about 160 pounds of tension on the neck. That's why they have a metal truss rod inside the neck, to keep it from snapping in half. Wood is strong, but it's not that strong. \n\nDifferent woods make different sounds, too. They call them tonewoods. Mahogany is warm, maple is bright. A lot of old violins, like the Stradivarius ones, were made from wood that grew during the Little Ice Age. The trees grew slower because it was so cold, so the wood is denser. That's why people think they sound so good. The climate really messes with everything. Like how the glaciers are melting and raising the sea levels. Miami is going to be underwater soon. They're already installing pumps just to keep the streets dry during high tide. It's wild that people still buy real estate there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't play guitar anymore. Did you know the universe is mostly dark matter? We can't even see it, but it makes up like 85% of the mass in the universe. It's just out there, holding galaxies together with its gravity, but it doesn't interact with light at all. It's completely invisible. Scientists only know it's there because of how it bends light from other stars. Gravitational lensing, they call it. Einstein predicted it way back when. Einstein was a genius, obviously, but he didn't even like the idea of quantum mechanics. He thought it was too random. \"God does not play dice with the universe,\" he said. \n\nBut it turns out, everything is kind of random at the subatomic level. Particles can be in two places at once until you look at them. Schrodinger's cat, right? The cat is alive and dead at the same time until you open the box. It only has value because the government says it does, and because we all agree to pretend it does. Most of it is just numbers on a computer screen anyway. \n\nComputers are everywhere now. They have to do that so they don't drown, because they have to consciously think about coming up to the surface to breathe. They don't have an automatic breathing reflex like we do. Reflexes are wild. Like when the doctor hits your knee with that little rubber hammer and your leg kicks out. That signal doesn't even go all the way to your brain, it just goes to your spinal cord and bounces right back. It's a survival mechanism. Spiders have crazy survival mechanisms, too. Molting sounds so uncomfortable, just crawling out of your own skeleton. Cicadas do that. They stay underground for 17 years just drinking tree sap, and then millions of them come out all at once, make a huge racket, and die a few weeks later. \n\nBut no, I've never had a week where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. No, no, I haven't wanted to die or anything like that. I've never really had those kinds of thoughts. \n\nThoughts are basically just electrical impulses, right? Like little sparks firing across synapses in your brain. Electricity is fascinating. Thomas Edison gets all the credit for the lightbulb, but Nikola Tesla was the one who really understood alternating current. Tesla was obsessed with the number three. He used to walk around a block three times before entering a building. Three is a powerful number in nature. You've got the three states of matter—solid, liquid, gas. Well, and plasma, I guess, if you count the sun. The sun is just a giant ball of plasma undergoing nuclear fusion. It converts millions of tons of hydrogen into helium every second. Helium is what makes balloons float, but we're actually running out of it on Earth. Once it escapes into the atmosphere, it just drifts off into space forever. Space is a vacuum, so sound can't travel through it. If a star explodes, it's completely silent.\n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any thoughts about dying or hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life—even just a few days—where your mind felt like it was racing with lots of ideas, or where you felt unusually confident or optimistic about things, or where you took on a lot of projects or goals all at once?\n\nPatient: Racing ideas? No, I don't think my mind has ever felt like it was racing like that. I mean, I think a lot, but not racing. I don't take on a bunch of projects either. I just do my job. \n\nJobs are funny things, really. We spend so much of our lives doing them. The word \"salary\" actually comes from the Latin word for salt, because Roman soldiers were sometimes paid in salt. Salt was super valuable back then. It preserves meat. Before refrigeration, if you didn't have salt, your food would just rot. Rotting is just bacteria breaking things down. Bacteria are everywhere. There are more bacteria cells in your body than human cells. You're basically just a walking ecosystem for microbes. Some of them live in extreme environments, like those hydrothermal vents at the bottom of the ocean. They call them extremophiles. They survive on chemicals instead of sunlight. Sunlight takes about eight minutes to reach the Earth. So if the sun just disappeared right now, we wouldn't even know it for eight minutes. We'd just be walking around in the daylight, totally oblivious. \n\nBut no, I've never had a time where I felt unusually confident or took on a lot of projects. Just my regular routine."
